---------- turn 18 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally pushed me to come in is this constant tightness. My neck and shoulders, and even my jaw, feel clenched almost all the time now. It’s like I’m bracing for bad news even when nothing’s happening—just sitting at my desk at school, or trying to watch TV in the evening.\n\nI kept thinking it was just posture, or age, or maybe too much marking papers… my students’ essays do seem to breed overnight sometimes. But it’s become hard to ignore. It’s just there, this tension, and I thought maybe I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Has this tension you're experiencing been interfering with your ability to do your job or other daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has, though I kept telling myself it was just a busy term. I’m a teacher, so there’s always prep and grading, and you know how it is, one stack of papers becomes three if you so much as turn your back on it. But lately I’ve actually been falling behind on lesson prep and marking in a way that isn’t normal for me.\n\nAnd outside of school, I usually help with community theater—organizing bits and pieces, keeping things running backstage, that sort of thing. I’ve had to give up parts of that because I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I didn’t like admitting that. It felt like dropping balls I used to manage without thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this tension is worse at certain times of the day or in particular situations?\n\nPatient: It’s… honestly, it feels like it’s most of the time now. I keep trying to pin it on a particular situation, like sitting at my desk too long, or the way I lean over papers—my wife says I hunch like one of those old monks copying manuscripts, which is probably not wrong—but even when I’m not doing anything demanding, it’s there.\n\nMy neck and shoulders stay tight, and my jaw too, like I’m clenching without realizing it. Even at night, just watching television, I catch myself sort of braced… as if I’m waiting for something bad to happen, though nothing actually is. So I don’t know that it’s only one time of day. It just doesn’t really let up much.\n\nDoctor: Are there any particular situations that make this tension feel even worse than usual?\n\nPatient: I suppose it gets worse when I’m sitting still, oddly enough—at my desk with papers in front of me, or in the evening when I’m just trying to watch television. You’d think relaxing would help, but that’s when I notice it most.\n\nMy neck and shoulders go tight, and my jaw sort of locks without me meaning to. It feels like I’m bracing myself for bad news, even though nothing is actually happening. I’ll be sitting there with a cup of tea, some harmless program on, and suddenly realize I’m wound up like I’m waiting outside the headmaster’s office. Funny, really, given I’m usually on the other side of that sort of thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge even when there's no apparent reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yes… quite often, actually. It’s not always tied to anything I can point at. I’ll just feel wound up most of the day, like I’m waiting for the next thing to go wrong.\n\nAt school I catch myself pacing around the classroom between periods, even when there’s nothing urgent happening. Or I’ll check my phone for messages from the contractor—far too many times, really—as if staring at it will somehow stop another problem appearing. I know that sounds silly, but it’s just this on-edge feeling that sits there even when there’s no obvious reason for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or exhausted?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. By the time the last bell rings, I feel absolutely wrung out—more than I think I should, really. It’s not just “long day at work” tired.\n\nAnd the annoying thing is, a decent night’s sleep doesn’t seem to put it right. Even a quiet Sunday, when I’ve done very little beyond make tea and potter about, doesn’t quite reset me. Then I’ll sit down with what should be a small stack of quizzes to mark, nothing dramatic, and it feels like pushing through mud.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any numbness or tingling sensations in your arms or legs?\n\nPatient: Numbness or tingling… no, I don’t think so. Not in any way I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, my legs might go a bit funny if I’ve sat marking for too long, but that’s more because I’ve been perched badly at the dining table for an hour and forgotten to move. My wife is always telling me I sit like a question mark. But no, not pins and needles coming out of nowhere or anything like that, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of this tension or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” at first. I tell myself I’m just prioritising, or that I’ll catch up later.\n\nBut I’ve fallen behind on lesson prep and grading more than I’m comfortable admitting. I’ll put off sitting down with it because I can feel myself tensing before I’ve even opened the folder. And I used to manage bits of the community theatre—sorting schedules, chasing people up, all that—but I’ve had to give some of that up. I just couldn’t keep all the plates spinning anymore.\n\nFunny, I used to enjoy the theatre side of things. There was this one production where the lighting cues were a complete disaster until the last rehearsal, and somehow that was less stressful than answering emails now. But yes… I have stepped back from things because I can’t seem to manage the strain of them like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s exactly it, really. I can tell myself, right, stop this now, you’ve got a class in front of you, focus on the worksheet or whatever it is—but then one small thing gets in.\n\nLike a late invoice, or a student who’s started slipping behind, and suddenly it’s not just that one thing. It becomes, well, what if I’ve missed something, what if it gets worse, what if someone asks and I haven’t got an answer… and it just runs on.\n\nIt’s a bit like when you pull one thread on a jumper and suddenly half the sleeve looks wrong. Sorry, that’s a strange comparison—my wife had me sorting out old clothes at the weekend and I got distracted by a moth hole in one of my cardigans. But yes, the thoughts do repeat, and I can’t seem to shut the chain of them down once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that you consider unreasonable or irrational, but that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I don’t know that I’d always call them irrational in the moment. Afterwards, maybe.\n\nIt can be something quite small, like a late invoice, or noticing one of the students has started falling behind a bit, and then my mind just… runs with it. I’ll be standing there trying to concentrate on the class in front of me, telling myself, come on, this is not the time, just get through the lesson—but the thought keeps branching off into what if this happens, what if I’ve missed that, what if someone asks and I don’t have an answer.\n\nIt’s ridiculous, really, because half the time nothing has actually happened yet. It’s like when you open one cupboard looking for a stapler and suddenly decide the whole kitchen needs reorganising—sorry, that’s not quite the same thing. But yes, I can know it’s probably out of proportion and still not be able to switch it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel detached from yourself or like you're observing yourself from outside your body?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not outside my body, no, I don’t think so. I mean, I can feel a bit on autopilot sometimes, like when I’m halfway through a lesson and suddenly realise I’ve left the register on the desk—actually, we changed the register system last term and it’s been a nuisance—but that’s not really what you mean, is it?\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m watching myself from the ceiling or anything like that. I haven’t noticed that, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're going through the motions of daily life without really being fully present or connected to what's happening around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I do get wrapped up in my own head sometimes, but whether that’s “not fully present” or just… being distracted, I don’t know.\n\nThere are days at school where I’m doing the usual routine—marking, corridors, saying the same thing about shirts tucked in for the hundredth time—and it can feel a bit mechanical. But then teaching is like that sometimes, isn’t it? You get into patterns. We’ve got this new timetable layout as well, which I still think is more confusing than the old one, though everyone pretends it’s an improvement.\n\nSo… I don’t think I feel disconnected exactly. Not in a strange way. More just busy or distracted, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your mood or interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really kept track of it like that. Mood-wise, I suppose I can be a bit flat some evenings, but then I’ve been teaching all day, and by the time you’ve answered emails and found out someone’s put the wrong worksheets in the Year 10 folder—sorry, that’s not really the question.\n\nActivities I normally enjoy… I still do things, I think. I mean, I don’t always get round to them, but that’s more time and energy than not wanting to. I wouldn’t say I’ve suddenly lost interest in everything. It’s hard to separate normal tiredness from anything else, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about everyday things, like work or finances, even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, more than I’d like to admit. It’s not always one big thing either, it’s like my mind won’t stay put.\n\nI’ll be thinking, did the contractors miss something in the house, and then suddenly I’m on to whether my lesson plans are slipping, or whether the robotics club kids are falling behind—though to be fair, some of them are more organised than the staff half the time. And then it becomes, am I letting my wife down, or the family, because I’m not keeping everything under control.\n\nThere doesn’t always have to be a clear reason, no. It just sort of finds something to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in activities that you normally enjoy over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a loss of pleasure, no. I still like the things I like, I think. It’s more that I don’t always get to them, or by the time I could, I end up pottering about or sorting something for school.\n\nLike, I’ll mean to sit down with something I enjoy, and then I remember there’s a form that needs chasing or some bit of planning I didn’t finish, and suddenly it’s half past ten. But if I actually do the thing, I don’t think I feel nothing from it.\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed a big change in that way, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced feelings of sadness or hopelessness over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say hopeless, no… not in the way that sounds. Sadness is harder to pin down. I have days where I feel worn down, or a bit low, but then you get to the end of a school day and someone’s lost the science cupboard key again, or a parent email comes in at quarter to six and it sort of colours everything.\n\nBut I don’t think I’ve been sitting there thinking everything’s hopeless. It’s more… tired and muddled, maybe. I’m not sure if that counts as sadness or just life being a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having intrusive thoughts that recur repeatedly and are difficult to control?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I don’t know if I’d call them intrusive exactly… it’s more like they barge in and then won’t leave.\n\nI can be in the middle of a lesson, trying to focus on the class in front of me, and one small thing pops up—like whether an invoice was paid late, or whether one of the quieter students is falling behind—and then suddenly it’s a whole chain of what-ifs. What if I’ve missed something, what if it becomes a bigger problem, what if someone’s expecting me to have sorted it already.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, right, stop it, you’re teaching now, get back to the room. But it doesn’t always work. It just keeps going in the background, like a radio left on in another room.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really kept track, to be honest. Sleep… I mean, it varies, but then it always has a bit during term time. You know what it’s like, or maybe you don’t, but school has this way of filling the evenings even when you’ve technically left the building.\n\nAppetite, I don’t think there’s been anything dramatic. I still eat meals, though sometimes dinner becomes a bit of toast or whatever’s easiest if I get in late or forget I was meant to cook. My wife used to say I could live on tea and biscuits during marking season, but that’s not exactly new.\n\nSo… no, I’m not sure I’d say there’s been a significant change. Nothing I’ve really noticed as a clear pattern.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

But it’s become hard to ignore. It’s just there, this tension, and I thought maybe I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Has this tension you're experiencing been interfering with your ability to do your job or other daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has, though I kept telling myself it was just a busy term. You’d think relaxing would help, but that’s when I notice it most.\n\nMy neck and shoulders go tight, and my jaw sort of locks without me meaning to. It feels like I’m bracing myself for bad news, even though nothing is actually happening. By the time the last bell rings, I feel absolutely wrung out—more than I think I should, really. It’s not just “long day at work” tired.\n\nAnd the annoying thing is, a decent night’s sleep doesn’t seem to put it right. Even a quiet Sunday, when I’ve done very little beyond make tea and potter about, doesn’t quite reset me. Then I’ll sit down with what should be a small stack of quizzes to mark, nothing dramatic, and it feels like pushing through mud.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any numbness or tingling sensations in your arms or legs?\n\nPatient: Numbness or tingling… no, I don’t think so. Not in any way I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, my legs might go a bit funny if I’ve sat marking for too long, but that’s more because I’ve been perched badly at the dining table for an hour and forgotten to move. My wife is always telling me I sit like a question mark. But no, not pins and needles coming out of nowhere or anything like that, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of this tension or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” at first. I tell myself I’m just prioritising, or that I’ll catch up later.\n\nBut I’ve fallen behind on lesson prep and grading more than I’m comfortable admitting. I’ll put off sitting down with it because I can feel myself tensing before I’ve even opened the folder. And I used to manage bits of the community theatre—sorting schedules, chasing people up, all that—but I’ve had to give some of that up. I just couldn’t keep all the plates spinning anymore.\n\nFunny, I used to enjoy the theatre side of things. There was this one production where the lighting cues were a complete disaster until the last rehearsal, and somehow that was less stressful than answering emails now. But yes… I have stepped back from things because I can’t seem to manage the strain of them like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s exactly it, really. I can tell myself, right, stop this now, you’ve got a class in front of you, focus on the worksheet or whatever it is—but then one small thing gets in.\n\nLike a late invoice, or a student who’s started slipping behind, and suddenly it’s not just that one thing. It becomes, well, what if I’ve missed something, what if it gets worse, what if someone asks and I haven’t got an answer… and it just runs on.\n\nIt’s a bit like when you pull one thread on a jumper and suddenly half the sleeve looks wrong. Sorry, that’s a strange comparison—my wife had me sorting out old clothes at the weekend and I got distracted by a moth hole in one of my cardigans. But yes, the thoughts do repeat, and I can’t seem to shut the chain of them down once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that you consider unreasonable or irrational, but that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I don’t know that I’d always call them irrational in the moment. Afterwards, maybe.\n\nIt can be something quite small, like a late invoice, or noticing one of the students has started falling behind a bit, and then my mind just… runs with it. I’ll be standing there trying to concentrate on the class in front of me, telling myself, come on, this is not the time, just get through the lesson—but the thought keeps branching off into what if this happens, what if I’ve missed that, what if someone asks and I don’t have an answer.\n\nIt’s ridiculous, really, because half the time nothing has actually happened yet. It’s like when you open one cupboard looking for a stapler and suddenly decide the whole kitchen needs reorganising—sorry, that’s not quite the same thing. But yes, I can know it’s probably out of proportion and still not be able to switch it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel detached from yourself or like you're observing yourself from outside your body?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not outside my body, no, I don’t think so. I mean, I can feel a bit on autopilot sometimes, like when I’m halfway through a lesson and suddenly realise I’ve left the register on the desk—actually, we changed the register system last term and it’s been a nuisance—but that’s not really what you mean, is it?\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m watching myself from the ceiling or anything like that. I haven’t noticed that, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're going through the motions of daily life without really being fully present or connected to what's happening around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I do get wrapped up in my own head sometimes, but whether that’s “not fully present” or just… being distracted, I don’t know.\n\nThere are days at school where I’m doing the usual routine—marking, corridors, saying the same thing about shirts tucked in for the hundredth time—and it can feel a bit mechanical. But then teaching is like that sometimes, isn’t it? You get into patterns. We’ve got this new timetable layout as well, which I still think is more confusing than the old one, though everyone pretends it’s an improvement.\n\nSo… I don’t think I feel disconnected exactly. Not in a strange way. More just busy or distracted, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your mood or interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really kept track of it like that. Mood-wise, I suppose I can be a bit flat some evenings, but then I’ve been teaching all day, and by the time you’ve answered emails and found out someone’s put the wrong worksheets in the Year 10 folder—sorry, that’s not really the question.\n\nActivities I normally enjoy… I still do things, I think. I mean, I don’t always get round to them, but that’s more time and energy than not wanting to. I wouldn’t say I’ve suddenly lost interest in everything. It’s hard to separate normal tiredness from anything else, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about everyday things, like work or finances, even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, more than I’d like to admit. It’s not always one big thing either, it’s like my mind won’t stay put.\n\nI’ll be thinking, did the contractors miss something in the house, and then suddenly I’m on to whether my lesson plans are slipping, or whether the robotics club kids are falling behind—though to be fair, some of them are more organised than the staff half the time. But if I actually do the thing, I don’t think I feel nothing from it.\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed a big change in that way, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced feelings of sadness or hopelessness over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say hopeless, no… not in the way that sounds. Sleep… I mean, it varies, but then it always has a bit during term time. I’m not sure I’d put it as consistently low most of the day, no. I mean, I have off days—everyone does, don’t they? Sometimes I’ll get home and feel a bit worn down, especially if the day’s been full of noise and small dramas, which in a school it usually is.

I’ve had to give up parts of that because I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I didn’t like admitting that. It felt like dropping balls I used to manage without thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this tension is worse at certain times of the day or in particular situations?\n\nPatient: It’s… honestly, it feels like it’s most of the time now. I keep trying to pin it on a particular situation, like sitting at my desk too long, or the way I lean over papers—my wife says I hunch like one of those old monks copying manuscripts, which is probably not wrong—but even when I’m not doing anything demanding, it’s there.\n\nMy neck and shoulders stay tight, and my jaw too, like I’m clenching without realizing it. Even at night, just watching television, I catch myself sort of braced… as if I’m waiting for something bad to happen, though nothing actually is. So I don’t know that it’s only one time of day. It just doesn’t really let up much.\n\nDoctor: Are there any particular situations that make this tension feel even worse than usual?\n\nPatient: I suppose it gets worse when I’m sitting still, oddly enough—at my desk with papers in front of me, or in the evening when I’m just trying to watch television. You’d think relaxing would help, but that’s when I notice it most.\n\nMy neck and shoulders go tight, and my jaw sort of locks without me meaning to. It feels like I’m bracing myself for bad news, even though nothing is actually happening. I’ll be sitting there with a cup of tea, some harmless program on, and suddenly realize I’m wound up like I’m waiting outside the headmaster’s office. Funny, really, given I’m usually on the other side of that sort of thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge even when there's no apparent reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yes… quite often, actually. It’s not always tied to anything I can point at. I’ll just feel wound up most of the day, like I’m waiting for the next thing to go wrong.\n\nAt school I catch myself pacing around the classroom between periods, even when there’s nothing urgent happening. Or I’ll check my phone for messages from the contractor—far too many times, really—as if staring at it will somehow stop another problem appearing. I know that sounds silly, but it’s just this on-edge feeling that sits there even when there’s no obvious reason for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or exhausted?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. By the time the last bell rings, I feel absolutely wrung out—more than I think I should, really. It’s not just “long day at work” tired.\n\nAnd the annoying thing is, a decent night’s sleep doesn’t seem to put it right. Even a quiet Sunday, when I’ve done very little beyond make tea and potter about, doesn’t quite reset me. Then I’ll sit down with what should be a small stack of quizzes to mark, nothing dramatic, and it feels like pushing through mud.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any numbness or tingling sensations in your arms or legs?\n\nPatient: Numbness or tingling… no, I don’t think so. Not in any way I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, my legs might go a bit funny if I’ve sat marking for too long, but that’s more because I’ve been perched badly at the dining table for an hour and forgotten to move. My wife is always telling me I sit like a question mark. But no, not pins and needles coming out of nowhere or anything like that, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of this tension or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” at first. I tell myself I’m just prioritising, or that I’ll catch up later.\n\nBut I’ve fallen behind on lesson prep and grading more than I’m comfortable admitting. I’ll put off sitting down with it because I can feel myself tensing before I’ve even opened the folder. And I used to manage bits of the community theatre—sorting schedules, chasing people up, all that—but I’ve had to give some of that up. I just couldn’t keep all the plates spinning anymore.\n\nFunny, I used to enjoy the theatre side of things. There was this one production where the lighting cues were a complete disaster until the last rehearsal, and somehow that was less stressful than answering emails now. But yes… I have stepped back from things because I can’t seem to manage the strain of them like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s exactly it, really. I can tell myself, right, stop this now, you’ve got a class in front of you, focus on the worksheet or whatever it is—but then one small thing gets in.\n\nLike a late invoice, or a student who’s started slipping behind, and suddenly it’s not just that one thing. It becomes, well, what if I’ve missed something, what if it gets worse, what if someone asks and I haven’t got an answer… and it just runs on.\n\nIt’s a bit like when you pull one thread on a jumper and suddenly half the sleeve looks wrong. Sorry, that’s a strange comparison—my wife had me sorting out old clothes at the weekend and I got distracted by a moth hole in one of my cardigans. But yes, the thoughts do repeat, and I can’t seem to shut the chain of them down once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that you consider unreasonable or irrational, but that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I don’t know that I’d always call them irrational in the moment. Afterwards, maybe.\n\nIt can be something quite small, like a late invoice, or noticing one of the students has started falling behind a bit, and then my mind just… runs with it. I’ll be standing there trying to concentrate on the class in front of me, telling myself, come on, this is not the time, just get through the lesson—but the thought keeps branching off into what if this happens, what if I’ve missed that, what if someone asks and I don’t have an answer.\n\nIt’s ridiculous, really, because half the time nothing has actually happened yet. It’s like when you open one cupboard looking for a stapler and suddenly decide the whole kitchen needs reorganising—sorry, that’s not quite the same thing. But yes, I can know it’s probably out of proportion and still not be able to switch it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel detached from yourself or like you're observing yourself from outside your body?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not outside my body, no, I don’t think so. I mean, I can feel a bit on autopilot sometimes, like when I’m halfway through a lesson and suddenly realise I’ve left the register on the desk—actually, we changed the register system last term and it’s been a nuisance—but that’s not really what you mean, is it?\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m watching myself from the ceiling or anything like that. I haven’t noticed that, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're going through the motions of daily life without really being fully present or connected to what's happening around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I do get wrapped up in my own head sometimes, but whether that’s “not fully present” or just… being distracted, I don’t know.\n\nThere are days at school where I’m doing the usual routine—marking, corridors, saying the same thing about shirts tucked in for the hundredth time—and it can feel a bit mechanical. But then teaching is like that sometimes, isn’t it? You get into patterns. We’ve got this new timetable layout as well, which I still think is more confusing than the old one, though everyone pretends it’s an improvement.\n\nSo… I don’t think I feel disconnected exactly. Not in a strange way. More just busy or distracted, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your mood or interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really kept track of it like that. Mood-wise, I suppose I can be a bit flat some evenings, but then I’ve been teaching all day, and by the time you’ve answered emails and found out someone’s put the wrong worksheets in the Year 10 folder—sorry, that’s not really the question.\n\nActivities I normally enjoy… I still do things, I think. I mean, I don’t always get round to them, but that’s more time and energy than not wanting to. I wouldn’t say I’ve suddenly lost interest in everything. It’s hard to separate normal tiredness from anything else, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about everyday things, like work or finances, even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, more than I’d like to admit. It’s not always one big thing either, it’s like my mind won’t stay put.\n\nI’ll be thinking, did the contractors miss something in the house, and then suddenly I’m on to whether my lesson plans are slipping, or whether the robotics club kids are falling behind—though to be fair, some of them are more organised than the staff half the time. And then it becomes, am I letting my wife down, or the family, because I’m not keeping everything under control.\n\nThere doesn’t always have to be a clear reason, no. It just sort of finds something to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in activities that you normally enjoy over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a loss of pleasure, no. I still like the things I like, I think. It’s more that I don’t always get to them, or by the time I could, I end up pottering about or sorting something for school.\n\nLike, I’ll mean to sit down with something I enjoy, and then I remember there’s a form that needs chasing or some bit of planning I didn’t finish, and suddenly it’s half past ten. But if I actually do the thing, I don’t think I feel nothing from it.\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed a big change in that way, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced feelings of sadness or hopelessness over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say hopeless, no… not in the way that sounds. Sadness is harder to pin down. I have days where I feel worn down, or a bit low, but then you get to the end of a school day and someone’s lost the science cupboard key again, or a parent email comes in at quarter to six and it sort of colours everything.\n\nBut I don’t think I’ve been sitting there thinking everything’s hopeless. It’s more… tired and muddled, maybe. I’m not sure if that counts as sadness or just life being a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having intrusive thoughts that recur repeatedly and are difficult to control?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I don’t know if I’d call them intrusive exactly… it’s more like they barge in and then won’t leave.\n\nI can be in the middle of a lesson, trying to focus on the class in front of me, and one small thing pops up—like whether an invoice was paid late, or whether one of the quieter students is falling behind—and then suddenly it’s a whole chain of what-ifs. What if I’ve missed something, what if it becomes a bigger problem, what if someone’s expecting me to have sorted it already.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, right, stop it, you’re teaching now, get back to the room. But it doesn’t always work. It just keeps going in the background, like a radio left on in another room.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really kept track, to be honest. Sleep… I mean, it varies, but then it always has a bit during term time. You know what it’s like, or maybe you don’t, but school has this way of filling the evenings even when you’ve technically left the building.\n\nAppetite, I don’t think there’s been anything dramatic. I still eat meals, though sometimes dinner becomes a bit of toast or whatever’s easiest if I get in late or forget I was meant to cook. My wife used to say I could live on tea and biscuits during marking season, but that’s not exactly new.\n\nSo… no, I’m not sure I’d say there’s been a significant change. Nothing I’ve really noticed as a clear pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed or had a loss of interest in activities most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not clearly, anyway. Two weeks sounds quite specific, and I haven’t really been marking it on a calendar.\n\nThere are stretches where I feel flat or fed up, especially when work piles up, but then something happens at school—a student says something funny, or there’s some ridiculous staffroom argument about who’s taken the last milk—and I’m sort of pulled back into it. So I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in everything most of the day.\n\nI still do things, I suppose. Maybe not with the same energy all the time, but… no, I’m not sure I can point to a solid two-week period like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt consistently low in mood for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as consistently low most of the day, no. I mean, I have off days—everyone does, don’t they? Sometimes I’ll get home and feel a bit worn down, especially if the day’s been full of noise and small dramas, which in a school it usually is.\n\nThere was this thing last week with the photocopier breaking right before year eleven needed exam papers, and everyone was acting like the world had ended. It’s silly, but those sorts of things can make the whole day feel heavier than it should.\n\nBut low in mood most of the day, for a period… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in that clear a way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
